0|dashboar | on the part of the health professional can translate into the avoidance or neglect of the sexual health education and services needs of people with disabilities. Thus, an important first step for the health professional is to recognize, acknowledge, and respect the right of all people, including people with physical and developmentally disabilities, to relevant and comprehensive sexual health education and services. Knowledge and Skills Develop the knowledge and skills to provide brief education and therapy: As noted above, there are a wide range of physical and developmental disabilities, each of which have specific and distinct implications for sexuality. Thus, health professionals can educate themselves about the relevant sexual health issues as they apply to the specific disabilities of their patients clients. Once a health professional is equipped with knowledge about the sexuality implications of a specific disability, they can become more confident and proactive in inquiring if their patients clients have sexual concerns. For example, a health professional who frequently works with patients who have recently acquired a disability (e.g., multiple sclerosis, spinal cord injury) or chronic illness (e.g., heart disease, cancer) can significantly increase the quality of care if they are equipped to ask and answer questions about sexuality during a routine assessment. Because sexuality is a sensitive or embarrassing topic for many people, many patients clients will not ask about the sexuality implications of their disability or medical condition unless they are invited to do so by their health care provider. When patients do have concerns related to sexuality, they will greatly appreciate being given the opportunity and encouragement to ask questions. Beginning a Dialogue About Sexuality with Youth with Disabilities It is important that we all take care of our sexual health. Many young people have questions about relationships, dating, and sex. Is it okay if I ask you a couple of questions about some of these things? Visit the Disability and Sexuality Resource Hub for examples of conversations guides and other resources that can be helpful to service providers: Di Giulio summarizes the PLISSIT model (permission, limited information, specific suggestions, intensive therapy) for providing basic sexual health education and therapy to people with disabilities. Deliver Education Where necessary, develop and deliver relevant sexual health education programs: Institutions (e.g., hospitals, long-term care facilities) and community service agencies that address the needs of people with disabilities can create and implement sexual health education programs tailored to the specific needs of their patients clients. The elicitation, intervention, evaluation process for applying the information, motivation, behavioural skills model to sexual health education recommended by the Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health can be used to develop effective programming for people with disabilities (We will looking more closely at using these concepts to provide effective sexual health education programs in the next module). Policy Development Promote organizational policy development: Many institutions and agencies serving people with disabilities do not have specific policies regarding sexuality. Policies related to sexuality may be particularly important for long-term care facilities or for agencies serving clients with highly restricted mobility. Without such policies, important sexuality issues may be addressed inconsistently, ineffectively, or not at all. The development of policies regarding sexuality not only contributes to greater consistency in how sexuality issues are addressed, they provide a basis upon which institutional agency administration, staff, clients, and client families can communicate and consult on issues related to sexuality, sexuality education, and sexual health services. Typically, policies regarding sexuality begin with statements of principle affirming the rights of people with disabilities to sexual expression and access to sexuality information. Applying the Principles of Disability Justice to Sexual Health Promotion with Youth with Disabilities Disability justice is a cross-disability framework that values individual differences and works to address the collective experience of disadvantage of marginalized groups to promote a society where all bodies and minds are valued. A disability justice framework can be applied to the sexual health promotion of youth with disabilities. Utilizing a disability justice approach, sexual health promotion with youth with disabilities should, Consider intersectionality (i.e., understanding how multiple social identities, such as race, gender identity, and sexual orientation, intersect to create unique experiences of advantage disadvantage for diverse youth with disabilities) Promote the leadership of those who are most impacted (i.e., valuing and prioritizing the perspectives of those who have been the most disadvantaged by societal systems and who understand the systems best through their lived experiences) Work to challenge ableist social norms, which do not account for the needs and experiences of people people with disabilities Commit to cross-movement organizing (i.e., working with other disadvantaged groups to address shared experiences of systemic disadvantages that contribute to sexual health inequities) Recognize wholeness (i.e., recognizing the many dimensions of a person, such as their experiences, strengths, and the many aspects of their identity, as well as the inherent worth of all individuals) Encourage each individual to honour their bodies, needs, and abilities Commit to cross-disability solidarity (i.e., working to address the collective experience of disadvantage that contribute to the sexual health inequities experienced by all members of the disability community) Promote interdependence (i.e., recognizing the value of reciprocal relationships and community support) Promote collective access (i.e., working to create spaces and systems where everyone s accessibility needs are considered and addressed) Leave no one behind (i.e., working to ensure that sexual health promotion accounts for and addresses the needs of all people with disabilities) Adapted from SIECCAN. . Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Promotion with Disabled Youth. Toronto, ON. Sex Information and Education Council of Canada . Staff and Family Educate staff and family members: In most cases, the successful implementation of an institutional agency policy regarding sexuality requires the provision of information sessions for clients, client family members (e.g., parents) and training seminars for staff. First and foremost, clients need to be included, where possible, in the policy development process and made aware of available education and clinic services. Family members should be informed of the policy and given the opportunity to attend information education sessions. Finally, it should be noted that most health professionals do not receive specific training, as part of their education, on providing sexual health education generally and even fewer receive training on providing sexuality education and services to people with disabilities. Staff members who have acquired knowledge and skills in this area can play an indispensable role in advocating for, and delivering, staff training sessions on sexuality and disability. Andrews, E. Required There is no required course reading this week. Course Review We have covered a lot of material over the past 11 weeks! Perhaps the most important message is that local government and politics is important to our well-being and quality of life. Here is a brief summary, week by week, of our progress in learning about local politics and government in Canada. Municipalities are corporations established by provinces to provide local representation and local services. They have powers set out in provincial legislation and are important as representative : Week 1 described the municipal position within a federal system where powers are divided between the national and provincial territorial governments. levels of government and as service providers. We reviewed the institutional approach, community power, social movements, political economy, and public choice theories and have applied these theories in our discussions to help us understand the complexity of municipal issues. Week 2 also introduced four key tensions that were expanded on through the course: Tension between efficiency and effectiveness Tension between service delivery and democratic roles Tensions related to municipal autonomy and the relationship with provinces and territories Tensions related to interests and how local governments balance the many interests they serve Over time, municipalities have been reviewed, revised, and reconfigured to better meet changing local needs as well as the political directions and expectations of the province. It is important to have a historical perspective, as past decisions have shaped (and constrained) current decisions about municipalities. The module introduced charter cities, considered whether there is a right size for municipalities, and showed that there is consistency in issues that challenge municipalities from the past and today, like housing and infrastructure. We considered the role of the mayor and councillors and the decision-making structure of council and different types of committees. The processes of municipal government affect who makes decisions and how they make them. The module looked at some key elements of municipal elections (term limits, ward vs. at-large elections, ward boundaries and size) as well as issues with the election process (ranked ballot, political parties). : Week 2 challenged you to think about the theoretical approaches that have informed your study of local government. : Week 3 provided an understanding of the evolution of local government in Canada from the early, pre-Confederation times, to the suburbanization of the 1950s and 60s, and on to the current times. : Week 4 looked at the machinery of local government with a focus on elected representation and governance structures and processes. It also explored challenges associated with representation and diversity. It is important to remember that the structure of municipal government is not firmly fixed amendments to municipal acts and governance reviews are undertaken from time to time. Municipal administration tends to be organized under a CAO or city manager, whereby some or all departments and functions report to the senior leader who in turn reports directly to council. Municipalities have one or more types of accountability officers, including an integrity commissioner, ombudsman, auditor general, lobbyist registrar (or registration process), and closed meeting investigation. These functions may be internal to the municipality and report directly to council or may be established by the province territory with responsibility for all municipalities. Municipalities establish special purpose bodies to take on tasks or functions that require specialized attention, like housing, hydro, the zoo, and transit. These corporations are owned by and accountable to the municipality and will have varying authority and autonomy from the municipality. In addition, provinces establish special purpose bodies to oversee or supplement municipalities, or they require the establishment of municipal special purpose bodies in legislation and regulations (e.g., police). Municipal-provincial relationships change over time depending on provincial and local politics and pressures. The informal federal-municipal relationship also changes over time, but it is always mediated by the fact that municipalities aren t a constitutionally recognized form of government. While municipalities continue to be at a disadvantage because of the lack of constitutional standing, there are new multi-governance paths for intergovernmental relationships that work around the lack of constitutional status. Not to be forgotten, municipalities also have longstanding formal and informal relationships with other municipalities. This is an important relationship for both parties, which can be expected to increase in importance in the future. Indigenous peoples are important to municipalities the Indigenous population is growing at a faster rate than non-Indigenous populations, they are younger than average, and they : Week 5 continued to explore the machinery of local government with a focus on the administration or bureaucracy, municipal accountability officers, and municipal special purpose bodies (or agencies, boards, and commissions (A, B, Cs)). : Week 6 focused on the complicated relationship between the municipal, provincial, and federal levels of government in Canada the often grey area of intergovernmental relations. : Week 7 examined municipal-Indigenous relationships. are increasingly urban. In addition, municipalities may be affected by formal land claims settlements negotiated by the federal and provincial territorial governments. The module explored three types of relationships: formal municipal-First Nations agreements, urban reserves, and other accords, protocols, and policy and program initiatives within the It provided some historical perspective on the traditional and imposed First Nations governance as well as examples of self-governance. The module considered municipal expenditures on directly controlled services, downloaded provincial services or regulated services, special purpose bodies, and capital spending. It looked at the three main sources of municipal revenues property taxes, user fees, and grants and funding from other levels of government. The module gave you an opportunity to try the difficult task of balancing a budget making budget decisions in the context of competing priorities and pressures. It highlighted the challenges facing municipalities and helped you to understand the options available to them. Municipalities are described as being in a state of fiscal crisis and the module considered possible responses. There are many internal and external influences on municipal policy; although, we may think of policy in terms of rational processes, it is far from the case in reality. and considered with input from interested stakeholders. Policy options are constrained (or enabled) by municipal formal powers, municipal staff, and financial capacity. Council committees, often with input from interested parties, discuss policy before it is debated and voted on at open council meetings. The module also considered issues related to municipal services, which can be delivered directly by municipal staff, municipal special purpose bodies, or for-profit or nonprofit organizations contracted to provide the services. Over time, municipalities have become more involved in non- traditional and non-property-related service issues, such as homelessness, poverty reduction, and climate change. : Week 8 explored the complex issue of municipal finance by looking at municipal budgets and budget processes. : Week 9 considered the municipal policy-making processes and environment and highlighted that policy-making is a complex process at the local level. : Week 10 considered municipal relationships with the public, including public participation and engagement as well as relationships with service users, citizens , and Many different interests and interest groups business, identity, local community, consumer, staff union, and policy groups try to influence municipal decision-making and service delivery. While there have been many strong efforts at engagement, municipal relationships with the public are not as inclusive or accessible as they need to be to connect effectively with diverse individuals, groups, and communities. Technology and online platforms have the potential for enhanced access and connection and greater levels of engagement. The module ended with a brief look at climate change and local government. This mini case study connected with the discussion of technology and globalization and provided an example of Your Turn Activity What course content resonated with you? As you review the key course learning summary on the previous page, you will no doubt notice some similarities and themes in what other students are taking away from the course as important learnings. Here are some additional thoughts on key course themes: : Week 11 looked at two important issues facing local government today technology and globalization. Both will shape the future path of municipalities within provinces, nationally, and on the international stage. They bring implications for relationships with other levels of government and for relationships with community members. Complex Environment Municipalities operate in complex environments. The structure of municipalities can be complicated with regional, two-tier structures as well as municipal and provincial special purpose bodies. Elected representatives are not associated with political parties, which can make decision-making more robust, but at the same time more complicated and more difficult to predict. Downloading has added complexity associated with provincial program requirements and funding and municipal funding, particularly grants, and is subject to change. In addition to special purpose bodies, municipalities have relationships with the private and nonprofit sectors to deliver services. Municipal issues cross boundaries and are increasingly regional. Technology and globalization add further complexities to the challenging context in which municipalities operate. Change Local government, unlike the constitutionally recognized provincial territorial or federal levels of government, is subject to change. While the foundation is remarkably similar to that of the Baldwin Act, 1849, there have been changes in the structure and responsibilities of municipalities through provincially imposed amalgamations and changes to representation and roles. Municipalities have been able to pursue some structural and governance reforms. However, more frequently, provincial decisions overlook municipal and public opposition to the changes. Relationships Municipalities have formal and informal relationships with the public and many interests, stakeholders, and levels of government. As the level of government closest to the people, these relationships bring high expectations regarding efficiency, effectiveness, representation, and engagement in decision- making processes. Municipalities face significant limitations in a formal relationship with the federal government, even though federal policies frequently have direct implications for municipalities. Municipal relationships with provinces are politically unstable and change happens in unanticipated forms. Relationships with Indigenous governments and communities have become increasingly important to municipalities. Capacity Municipalities have limited financial and jurisdictional capacity to address the increasingly complicated local challenges and issues. The course looked at a number of ways that the lack of financial capacity can be addressed, but action has been limited. Lack of jurisdictional capacity was a recurring theme throughout the course. Although municipalities can engage in multi-level governance, act as eager beavers, and be innovative in relationships with the private sector and civil society, these workarounds take time and effort. Looking Back at the Four Key Tensions Before we leave this review and reflection module, let s reflect on the four tensions from the Introducing Four Key Tensions page in Module 2. These tensions have been evident throughout the course, in the discussions of structure, reforms, finance, delegated roles and authority, interests, representation, service delivery, and policy. 1. Tensions between the municipal service delivery and democratic roles were presented in the discussion of role, structure, and process and repeated in the other tensions below. As urban municipalities continue to grow in population and economic importance, and as issues become increasingly complex, municipalities will need to play a stronger democratic and representative role. 2. Tensions between efficiency and effectiveness were evident in the discussions of municipal role, processes, and finances. Cost efficiency is key if the role of local government is service provision; whereas effectiveness and how municipalities do their work and make decisions is critical to the municipal role of democratic representation. The tension between efficiency and effectiveness isn t either or because local governments need some elements of both; the tension is in the balance between efficiency and effectiveness. 3. Tensions about municipal autonomy and the relationship with provinces and territories have been evident throughout the course from the historical overview early in the course to the last module and the future path for municipalities. The municipal relationship with the federal government is constrained by lack of constitutional autonomy and politics at all levels. 4. Finally, the course explored tensions related to stakeholders and how local governments balance and mediate these interests. Reflecting on the discussion of interests, perhaps we also need to add represent to this tension, i.e., tensions related to representation, interests, and how local governments , to highlight tensions in the selection of elected officials, how they represent the many interests in their communities, and whether they play trustee or delegate roles. We are now at the end of the course. At this point, you have developed an appreciation for the often- overlooked local level of government and now possess a better understanding of the challenges of the current system and structures of governance. You also understand how your municipality is governed and have seen the municipality in action in a decision-making council or committee meeting. You can apply these course learnings to future engagement with your municipality and to your broader understanding of inter-governmental relationships in Canada. In addition, you should also have a good understanding of the many municipal interests and the challenges that municipalities face in trying to balance these varied interests. The course explored a number of issues facing municipalities as well as opportunities for leadership, innovation, and continued strengthening of local government. Understand the roles of Information, Motivation, and Behavioural Skills in changing sexual health related behaviour. 2. Understand the roles of Elicitation, Intervention, and Evaluation in applying the IMB model to sexual health promotion activities. 3. Apply the Elicitation, Intervention, and Evaluation framework and the Information, Motivation, and Behavioural Skills model to sexual health promotion activities. 4. Become aware of opportunities for further professional development in the area of human sexuality. Topic Overview The primary objectives of this course have been to encourage a critical understanding of the social context of human sexuality and to introduce key theories and concepts relevant to the study of human sexuality. We have also paid particular attention to how these concepts can be applied to the work of health professionals addressing sexuality issues in their work. As we have examined the developmental tasks of each phase of the life-cycle and topics such as gender, sexual orientation, and disability, it has become evident that sexuality and sexual health are important components of individual and community well-being. One of the outcomes of the gradual transition from a Restrictive to a Permissive sexual ideology in Western culture is that it is increasingly accepted that in order to comprehensively and holistically meet the needs of their constituents, health professionals (e.g., nurses, rehabilitation counsellors, social workers, and educators) must be willing and able to address issues of sexuality. If we are to utilize our knowledge and expertise in human sexuality to the benefit of our students, clients and communities, it is crucial to incorporate effective, well-tested approaches to sexual health promotion in our work. The aim of this final module is to provide you with a basic general approach to developing and implementing effective sexual health promotion activities. Please Note To effectively promote sexual health, health professionals will benefit from using well-tested, behaviourally focused approaches to sexual health enhancement and problem prevention. We are at a unique juncture in history and have a rare opportunity to develop global, national, and community strategies to promote sexual health for the new century. This opportunity has been created by the fact that the world is experiencing a new sexual revolution and a public health imperative. Much like the sexual revolution of the 1960s and 1970s, it is a revolution fuelled by incredible scientific advances, as well as a dramatic social and economic change We also face a myriad of sexual health problems, which is creating an enormous burden on societies. These two factors are putting pressure on health ministries to develop comprehensive approaches to sexual health promotion . The Information, Motivation, Behavioural Skills Model for Sexual and Reproductive Health Promotion In Module 8, Part 2, we saw how the information, motivation, behavioural skills theoretical model can be applied to adolescent sexuality education. Although it was initially developed and tested as a theoretical model to guide interventions targeting HIV AIDS sexual risk reduction, the IMB model can be used to guide a wide range of sexual health promotion and education activities . Whether we are creating and implementing programs to promote sexual health with individuals, groups, or communities, the IMB model provides a basic and well-tested conceptual framework for ensuring that our interventions are effective in reaching their objectives. First and foremost, sexual and reproductive health behaviors, whether they involve sexual function promotion, contraceptive utilization, STD HIV prevention, reproductive cancer screening, or sexual adaptations to aging, illness, or disability, represent sexualized behavioral events. Each of these sexual and reproductive health behaviors has acquired sexual meaning as a result of social ascription, individual learning, and the sexual and reproductive biology and physiology involved in all of the acts and adjustments in question. As sexualized acts, sexual and reproductive behaviors are all influenced by a common set of determinants of sexual behavior, including information, motivation, and behavioral skills factors, and all are potentially modifiable via common intervention pathways focusing on changing these factors in efforts to promote sexual and reproductive health (Fisher and Fisher, 1998, p. s 39-40). The IMB Model for Sexual Health Promotion Acquisition of Information Individual must learn information that is relevant to the practice of sexual and reproductive health behaviour. Development of Motivation Individual must become personally motivated and or socially motivated to practice sexual and reproductive health behaviour. Development of Behavioural Skills Individual must learn the actual behavioural skills to perform sexual and reproductive health behaviour. Although the necessity of including relevant information, motivation, and behavioural skills in sexual health promotion programs seems obvious, very few existing programs actually contain all three elements. In many cases, sexual health education programs have been exclusively information-based and, in particular, have neglected to provide specific behavioural skills training. Key Point The degree to which an individual is able to enact sexual health promoting behaviour will be dependant to a significant extent on their level of relevant information, motivation, and behavioural skills. Case Example An IMB Model Intervention Approach to Assist Women with Sexual Function Difficulties Related to Chemotherapy Treatment for Breast Cancer. Research suggests that difficulty with sexual functioning is one of the most distressing problems for long-term survivors of breast cancer and that in many cases these problems are related to vaginal dryness resulting from chemotherapy treatment (e.g., see Broeckel, Thors, Jacobsen, Small, and Cox, 2002). Therefore, one of the objectives of a sexual health education intervention for this group might be to target information, motivation, and behaviour skills related to the use of vaginal lubrication prior to sexual activity. Information: (Must be relevant, complete, and easy to translate into behaviour) Examples of relevant information: Many women treated for breast cancer experience problems with sexual function. These problems are often associated with vaginal dryness. Methods to relieve vaginal dryness, including the use of lubricants prior to sexual activity, can be used to improve sexual function among women treated for breast cancer. Lubricants (e.g. Astroglide) are available, without prescription, at the pharmacy in the Family Planning section where the condoms are displayed. Motivation: (Must help the recipient develop a positive attitude towards the behaviour by recognizing that she will benefit from engaging in the behaviour personal motivation and that there is some form of external support for engaging in the behaviour social motivation ). Examples of motivational messages: Restoring sexual function and enjoyment can play an important role in regaining an overall sense of well-being following treatment for breast cancer (personal motivation). Applying a lubricant prior to sexual activity can be an effective and easy to use method for helping to restore sexual function (personal motivation). Other women experiencing vaginal dryness report that using lubricants is very helpful and health professionals recommend their use for this purpose (social motivation). Behavioural Skills:(Must include the teaching of the specific behavioural skills needed to initiate and maintain the behaviour). Examples of behavioural skills training: Instruction on how to locate lubricant in the pharmacy. Instruction on the application of vaginal lubrication (encourage practice and experimentation). Discussion and decision-making on when in the sexual script lubricant will be applied. Practice and rehearsal of communication with partner about the use of lubricants (give explicit and clear examples of communication and or incorporate role-playing exercises where participants practice bringing up and discussing the use of lubricants with a partner). Applying the IMB Model: Elicitation, Intervention, Evaluation Effectively applying the IMB model to a sexual health promotion intervention involves a basic three-step process: elicitation, intervention, and evaluation. In the elicitation phase, program planners assess or measure the target audience s information, motivation, and behavioural skills related to a specific set of sexual health behaviours. This can be done through focus groups, assessment interviews with clients, or questionnaires. In other words, conducting elicitation research will allow program planners to identify which aspects of information, motivation, and behavioural skills the target audience is lacking. Instead of creating programs based on our own assumptions or best guesses as to what the target audience needs, conducting elicitation research helps to ensure that an intervention will be relevant and have the desired impact. In the second step , the program is created and implemented based on the information, motivation, behavioural skills deficits weaknesses of the target audience. In the third step, the program is periodically assessed (again using focus groups, interviews, questionnaires, ect) to ensure that the program is successfully impacting on the information, motivation, and behavioural skills of the target audience. This information can be used to refine and improve the intervention. Key Point The comprehensive, systematic application of an IMB-based sexual health promotion program involves a three-step process of elicitation research, intervention, and evaluation. This program of information motivation behavioural skills and elicitation intervention evaluation will give you a basic structural framework for the delivery of effective sexual health promotion activities. Your required reading for this module is a section of the Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Education. This section, titled Key Components provides further description and discussion of the IMB model. The Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Education are a recommended resource support document for health professionals involved in the provision of sexual health education and promotion programs. Key Point The Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Education provide support and guidance for the development and implementation of effective sexual health education and promotion initiatives. Opportunities for Further Professional Development in the Area of Sexuality The field of sexology has advanced significantly in recent decades. Parallel to the increasing openness towards sexuality in Western culture, the importance of research on human sexuality and sexual health promotion has been increasingly recognized. In addition, as we have seen throughout this course, addressing sexuality and sexual health issues is also gradually becoming an accepted component of the practice of a wide variety of helping professions. However, despite these advancements, it is also important to be aware that sexology has not yet reached the status of a distinct academic discipline. For example, few universities have sexology departments. Indeed, at present, only one sexology department exists in Canada, located at the University of Quebec at Montreal (UQAM s sexology programs are given in the french language). Several universities in Canada offer programs that enable students to specialize in human sexuality (e.g., Graduate programs in the Department of Family Studies at the University of Guelph; Interdisciplinary Studies in Sexuality, Marriage and the Family at the University of Waterloo). For most students seeking to become specialists in sexuality research, education, counselling or therapy, the most advantagious route to so is to pursue graduate studies in an appropriate related academic program (e.g., nursing, clinical, counselling or educational psychology, medicine, social work, education, ect.) and, to the extent possible, focus one s research and study on sexuality within that program. Many graduate programs have faculty members with an interest in sexology and who teach courses related to sexuality. Obtaining additional credentials in one of these more traditional disciplines also provides a broader qualification than training limited to sexology. This is an important factor to consider in choosing a graduate program. In sum, if you want to become a sexuality educator, become an educator first, if you want to become a medical sexologist, become a physician or nurse first, if you want to become a sex therapist, become a therapist first. In other words, first and foremost, establish your credentials in your discipline as you pursue your focus on sexuality. Becoming a Sex Therapist Technically, there are no licensing bodies in Canada that legally certify a person as a sex therapist. Generally speaking, a well-qualified sex therapist has advanced graduate training in an appropriate discipline such as medicine, psychology, social work, nursing ect. as well as additional specific training in sex therapy. Specific training in sex therapy is available through organizations such as the American Association of Sex Educators, Counselors, and Therapists and the Association of Sex Therapy in Ontario (ASTO: A Final Word This course has been ambitious in its scope and objectives. You have been asked to not only develop a critical understanding of the social context of human sexuality. You have also been asked to acquire a wide-ranging knowledge of sexuality issues and concepts that are crucial to the effective promotion of sexual health. You have taken an important first step in developing the knowledge and skills to make a meaningful contribution to the sexual health of those you are or will be working with. Enhancing the ability of individuals, couples, families, and communities to achieve healthy sexuality, a fundamental component of human well-being, is something to be proud of. All nursing homes should have policies that include the recognition that nursing home residents have a basic right to sexual expression just like any other person. This will make life more difficult and complicated for the staff but they have to understand and accept that dealing with these issues is part of the job. 0|dashboar | ans, respiratory (including speech organs), cardiovascular, reproductive, digestive, genito-urinary, blood and lymphatic, skin, and endocrine. Examples of physical disabilities that can affect sexuality include Multiple Sclerosis, Cerebral Palsy, Spinal Cord Injuries, and Sensory Disabilities. Definition of the term \"Developmental Disability\" The term \"developmental disability\" commonly applies to conditions that first occur before the age of 18 and affect a person's ability to learn and apply conceptual, social, and practical skills in everyday life. Autism spectrum disorder is an example of a developmental disability. Note that disabilities that impair cognitive function can also begin in adulthood (Sometimes referred to as \"intellectual disabilities\"). Key Point Million's of Canadians live with either physical or developmental disabilities. It is therefore important for health professionals to have an understanding of the inter-connection of disability and sexuality and to provide education to enable people with disabilities to maximize their sexual health and well-being. On a broader level, an examination of the topic of sexuality and disability provides a unique opportunity to deepen our understanding of both the social context of human sexuality and the nature of sexuality itself. As we have seen in this course, we all carry with us a set of assumptions about what constitutes \"normal\", \"healthy\" or \"natural\" sexuality. The traditional sexual script and media representations of sexuality and sexual behaviour have given us a very narrow conception of \"normal\". People with disabilities sometimes do not fit damaging societal standards of sexual attractiveness and beauty. People with disabilities, then, are often forced to adapt their sexual expression to their circumstances in ways that fundamentally challenge our assumptions about \"normal\" sexuality and sexual response. Ableism and Sexual Health \"Traditional ideas regarding disability and sexuality are often profoundly sexist, ableist, and heteronormative. Moreover, commonly held ideas regarding disability are medicalized or ableist understandings that view disability as an unfortunate, individual, biomedical problem that requires treatment or rehabilitation\" . Ableism is a way of thinking based on negative beliefs (e.g., false assumptions and stereotypes) and feelings (e.g., discomfort, negative attitudes) about people with disabilities. Impact of ableism on the sexual health and well-being of disabled youth False assumptions being made about the ability and desire of disabled youth to date, be in relationships, have sexual experiences, and become parents; Not having discussions about sexuality and sexual health with disabled youth because disabled youth are believed to not have the capacity to make their own decisions about their sexuality; The sexual health needs and experiences of disabled youth not being included in sexual health education programs; Sexual and reproductive health services not being offered to disabled youth because they are believed to be irrelevant to them; and Not respecting the right of disabled youth to autonomously make decisions about their own sexual health and well-being. SIECCAN . If you are interested in understanding the sexual health education promotion needs of youth with disabilities see The Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Promotion with Disabled Youth and Sexual Health Promotion: A Service Provider Guide for Working with Disabled Youth Key Point The topic of sexuality and disability demonstrates the variability in \"normal\" sexual response expression. As both Campbell and Di Giulio make clear, perhaps the most important lesson to be learned from an examination of this topic is that often the greatest obstacle to healthy sexual expression for a person with a disability is not the disability itself but rather the attitudes towards the sexuality of people with disabilities held by family, caregivers, and society in general. As we have seen throughout this course, sexuality represents a fundamental component of who we are as human beings and this is equally the case for people with physical or developmental disabilities. However, among the common misperceptions about people with disabilities is that they are either asexual, without sexual desires or sexual response or, in the case of people with developmental disabilities, that they are incapable of responsible sexual behaviour or healthy relationships. In sum, people with disabilities not only have to overcome the limitations that a disability may place upon their sexual expression, they must do so in a social environment that discourages or ignores their sexuality. If you are interested in understanding the sexual health education promotion needs of Autistic youth see The Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Promotion with Autistic Youth and Sexual Health Promotion: A Service Provider Guide for Working with Autistic Youth Key Point The person with a disability faces the challenge of living in a society that holds many myths and misperceptions about sexuality and disability. Sexuality and Disability: Myths and Misperceptions As educators working towards positive social change, it is important that we seek to challenge and disrupt destructive and unjust ableist myths and misperceptions related to sexuality and disability. Unfortunately, the myths and misperceptions related to sexuality and disability listed below are still evident in our society. Physical Disability People with physical disabilities do not have the same levels of sexual desire and feelings as people without disabilities. People with physical disabilities have given up the sexual part of themselves. People with disabilities such as spinal cord injury are not capable of pleasurable and satisfying sexual experiences. Youth and adults with physical disabilities are not sexually active. Youth and adults with physical disabilities do not want or need sexuality education. Developmental Disability People with developmental disabilities are child-like in their sexual desires and behaviour. People with developmental disabilities should be discouraged from being sexual because they cannot control their sexual impulses. Youth with developmental disabilities should not receive sexuality education because it will lead to inappropriate behaviour. People with developmental disabilities are incapable of healthy sexual and romantic relationships. Contrary to prevailing myths and misconceptions, many disabled youth are interested in and or are engaging in sexual activity. In a survey of youth (ages 16 24) in Canada, about two-thirds of youth with disabilities reported having been sexually active: 65 had touched a partner s genitals for pleasure; 59 have had penis-vagina sex; 42 have had penis-anal sex, and 58 have had oral sex . The sexual activity of people with disabilities can look and feel different than for non-disabled people and these ways of engaging in sexual activity have traditionally been excluded from dominant sexual scripts . When people with disabilities internalize these dominant sexual scripts that do not account for or align with their needs and wants, sexual experiences can be unfulfilling. Sexuality and Disability: The Self-Fulfilling Prophecy The acceptance of these myths and misconceptions can lead to a self-fulfilling prophecy in which people with disabilities either internalize negative societal attitudes towards sexuality and disability or they live in environments in which healthy sexual expression is impossible. In other words, if society sends the consistent message that people with disabilities are not fully sexual or are incapable of satisfying, healthy sexual relationships, people with disabilities themselves may come to accept and be influenced by these destructive messages. Key Point Negative societal messages about sexuality and disability can lead to a self-fulfilling prophecy in which people with disabilities internalize these messages and retreat from intimacy and sexuality. For example, while physical disabilities, such as spinal cord injury, may place limits on sexual behaviour and response, feelings of sexual inadequacy and poor self-esteem are, in many respects, rooted in the acceptance of false societal attitudes and beliefs suggesting that people who use wheel chairs are less sexual. As a person in a wheel chair absorbs, and perhaps accepts, these negative messages, they may become sufficiently discouraged about the prospect of leading a satisfying sexual life that sexual feelings are avoided. When self-esteem falls and a negative body image develops, sexual desires and feelings may decline. This is particularly unfortunate given that restoring a sense of sexuality and positive feelings about being sexual can play an important role in helping people recovering from severe injury or illness rebuild a sense of well-being and quality of life. Prevailing attitudes towards, and the treatment of, people with developmental disabilities provides an even more dramatic example of this self-fulfilling prophecy. Often, people with developmental disabilities live in environments in which they are never alone or have the privacy for sexual expression. As a result, with no opportunity for privacy, any sexual behaviour or expression will occur in an inappropriate context. For example, a couple may have sex in a park because there is no opportunity to be alone in the group home where they live and sexual interaction is not allowed in the home. If they are caught, their behaviour will be seen as evidence that they are not capable of responsible sexual behaviour rather than as a possible indication that their need for intimacy and a lack of privacy led them to the park. Hingsburger and Tough give the example of an assessment test that was used with youth with developmental disabilities to illustrate how they are sometimes pushed to behave in ways that seem inappropriate but are in fact quite logical. The test asks the individual with a disability to Point to where it is OK to have sex. The individual is then shown a series of four pictures: a workplace, a park, a bedroom, and a bathroom. The correct answer, the bedroom, seems obvious. A person answering by pointing to any picture, other than that of the bedroom, is marked incorrect. Yet this question shows the naivete of many who do not understand real-world conditions for people with disabilities. Many people with disabilities point to the park. When it is suggested that the bedroom is the right answer, the individual often looks incredulously at the instructor. The bedroom, one person responded, we can t have sex in our bedrooms, we d get killed! For this man and his girlfriend, the park was where they went to engage in intimate behavior. It was private (sort of), safe (kind of), and away from eyes of staff and others who might punish . As Di Guilio points out, developmentally disabled youth can often benefit from enhanced social skills training as part of the sexuality education that they require. Yet, as we have seen, many youth with developmental disabilities do not receive appropriate sexuality education but instead absorb sex negative messages designed to prevent sexual expression. The Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Education offer the following guidance with respect to short and long-term care facilities serving youth, adults, and seniors with disabilities: Children, youth, adults, and seniors with developmental, intellectual, and physical disabilities, as well as people with chronic illnesses, often lack access to sexual health education relevant to their specific needs. Every person has individual needs, strengths, and challenges. Comprehensive sexual health education should be adapted to clients' specific learning needs. Personnel in these facilities can be provided with opportunities for in-service and continuing education to increase their capacity to provide comprehensive sexual health education. Staff should be trained to connect individuals to appropriate services . Toward Healthy Sexuality Among People with Disabilities: The Role of the Health Professional Myths and misperceptions surrounding the sexuality of people with disabilities are wide spread and in many cases health professionals are not immune to these biases. To the extent that health professionals assume that sexual desires and expression are either absent or inappropriate for people with disabilities, they may perpetuate and increase the barriers to healthy sexuality that the disabled already face. Key Point Many health professionals hold common myths and misperceptions about sexuality and disability and are uncomfortable addressing sexuality issues in their work. Comprehensive health care for people with disabilities should include the identification and removal of barriers to sexual health and the provision of sexual health education and services that will enable youth and adults with disabilities to enhance their sexual health and avoid sexual health problems. Listed below are five basic steps that health professionals can take to promote the sexual health of people with disabilities. Acknowledge and Respect Acknowledge and respect the right of people with disabilities to sexual health education and clinical services: On a general level, many health professionals are uncomfortable addressing sexuality issues with patients clients and this discomfort may be particularly acute with respect to people with disabilities. This discomfort on the part of the health professional can translate into the avoidance or neglect of the sexual health education and services needs of people with disabilities. Thus, an important first step for the health professional is to recognize, acknowledge, and respect the right of all people, including people with physical and developmentally disabilities, to relevant and comprehensive sexual health education and services. Knowledge and Skills Develop the knowledge and skills to provide brief education and therapy: As noted above, there are a wide range of physical and developmental disabilities, each of which have specific and distinct implications for sexuality. Thus, health professionals can educate themselves about the relevant sexual health issues as they apply to the specific disabilities of their patients clients. Once a health professional is equipped with knowledge about the sexuality implications of a specific disability, they can become more confident and proactive in inquiring if their patients clients have sexual concerns. For example, a health professional who frequently works with patients who have recently acquired a disability (e.g., multiple sclerosis, spinal cord injury) or chronic illness (e.g., heart disease, cancer) can significantly increase the quality of care if they are equipped to ask and answer questions about sexuality during a routine assessment. Because sexuality is a sensitive or embarrassing topic for many people, many patients clients will not ask about the sexuality implications of their disability or medical condition unless they are invited to do so by their health care provider. When patients do have concerns related to sexuality, they will greatly appreciate being given the opportunity and encouragement to ask questions. Beginning a Dialogue About Sexuality with Youth with Disabilities It is important that we all take care of our sexual health. Many young people have questions about relationships, dating, and sex. Is it okay if I ask you a couple of questions about some of these things? Visit the Disability and Sexuality Resource Hub for examples of conversations guides and other resources that can be helpful to service providers: Di Giulio summarizes the PLISSIT model (permission, limited information, specific suggestions, intensive therapy) for providing basic sexual health education and therapy to people with disabilities. Deliver Education Where necessary, develop and deliver relevant sexual health education programs: Institutions (e.g., hospitals, long-term care facilities) and community service agencies that address the needs of people with disabilities can create and implement sexual health education programs tailored to the specific needs of their patients clients. The elicitation, intervention, evaluation process for applying the information, motivation, behavioural skills model to sexual health education recommended by the Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health can be used to develop effective programming for people with disabilities (We will looking more closely at using these concepts to provide effective sexual health education programs in the next module). Policy Development Promote organizational policy development: Many institutions and agencies serving people with disabilities do not have specific policies regarding sexuality. Policies related to sexuality may be particularly important for long-term care facilities or for agencies serving clients with highly restricted mobility. Without such policies, important sexuality issues may be addressed inconsistently, ineffectively, or not at all. The development of policies regarding sexuality not only contributes to greater consistency in how sexuality issues are addressed, they provide a basis upon which institutional agency administration, staff, clients, and client families can communicate and consult on issues related to sexuality, sexuality education, and sexual health services. Typically, policies regarding sexuality begin with statements of principle affirming the rights of people with disabilities to sexual expression and access to sexuality information. Applying the Principles of Disability Justice to Sexual Health Promotion with Youth with Disabilities Disability justice is a cross-disability framework that values individual differences and works to address the collective experience of disadvantage of marginalized groups to promote a society where all bodies and minds are valued. A disability justice framework can be applied to the sexual health promotion of youth with disabilities. Utilizing a disability justice approach, sexual health promotion with youth with disabilities should, Consider intersectionality (i.e., understanding how multiple social identities, such as race, gender identity, and sexual orientation, intersect to create unique experiences of advantage disadvantage for diverse youth with disabilities) Promote the leadership of those who are most impacted (i.e., valuing and prioritizing the perspectives of those who have been the most disadvantaged by societal systems and who understand the systems best through their lived experiences) Work to challenge ableist social norms, which do not account for the needs and experiences of people people with disabilities Commit to cross-movement organizing (i.e., working with other disadvantaged groups to address shared experiences of systemic disadvantages that contribute to sexual health inequities) Recognize wholeness (i.e., recognizing the many dimensions of a person, such as their experiences, strengths, and the many aspects of their identity, as well as the inherent worth of all individuals) Encourage each individual to honour their bodies, needs, and abilities Commit to cross-disability solidarity (i.e., working to address the collective experience of disadvantage that contribute to the sexual health inequities experienced by all members of the disability community) Promote interdependence (i.e., recognizing the value of reciprocal relationships and community support) Promote collective access (i.e., working to create spaces and systems where everyone s accessibility needs are considered and addressed) Leave no one behind (i.e., working to ensure that sexual health promotion accounts for and addresses the needs of all people with disabilities) Adapted from SIECCAN. . Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Promotion with Disabled Youth. Toronto, ON. Sex Information and Education Council of Canada . Staff and Family Educate staff and family members: In most cases, the successful implementation of an institutional agency policy regarding sexuality requires the provision of information sessions for clients, client family members (e.g., parents) and training seminars for staff. First and foremost, clients need to be included, where possible, in the policy development process and made aware of available education and clinic services. Family members should be informed of the policy and given the opportunity to attend information education sessions. Finally, it should be noted that most health professionals do not receive specific training, as part of their education, on providing sexual health education generally and even fewer receive training on providing sexuality education and services to people with disabilities. Staff members who have acquired knowledge and skills in this area can play an indispensable role in advocating for, and delivering, staff training sessions on sexuality and disability. Andrews, E. Required There is no required course reading this week. Course Review We have covered a lot of material over the past 11 weeks! Perhaps the most important message is that local government and politics is important to our well-being and quality of life. Here is a brief summary, week by week, of our progress in learning about local politics and government in Canada. Municipalities are corporations established by provinces to provide local representation and local services. They have powers set out in provincial legislation and are important as representative : Week 1 described the municipal position within a federal system where powers are divided between the national and provincial territorial governments. levels of government and as service providers. We reviewed the institutional approach, community power, social movements, political economy, and public choice theories and have applied these theories in our discussions to help us understand the complexity of municipal issues. Week 2 also introduced four key tensions that were expanded on through the course: Tension between efficiency and effectiveness Tension between service delivery and democratic roles Tensions related to municipal autonomy and the relationship with provinces and territories Tensions related to interests and how local governments balance the many interests they serve Over time, municipalities have been reviewed, revised, and reconfigured to better meet changing local needs as well as the political directions and expectations of the province. It is important to have a historical perspective, as past decisions have shaped (and constrained) current decisions about municipalities. The module introduced charter cities, considered whether there is a right size for municipalities, and showed that there is consistency in issues that challenge municipalities from the past and today, like housing and infrastructure. We considered the role of the mayor and councillors and the decision-making structure of council and different types of committees. The processes of municipal government affect who makes decisions and how they make them. The module looked at some key elements of municipal elections (term limits, ward vs. at-large elections, ward boundaries and size) as well as issues with the election process (ranked ballot, political parties). : Week 2 challenged you to think about the theoretical approaches that have informed your study of local government. : Week 3 provided an understanding of the evolution of local government in Canada from the early, pre-Confederation times, to the suburbanization of the 1950s and 60s, and on to the current times. : Week 4 looked at the machinery of local government with a focus on elected representation and governance structures and processes. It also explored challenges associated with representation and diversity. It is important to remember that the structure of municipal government is not firmly fixed amendments to municipal acts and governance reviews are undertaken from time to time. Municipal administration tends to be organized under a CAO or city manager, whereby some or all departments and functions report to the senior leader who in turn reports directly to council. Municipalities have one or more types of accountability officers, including an integrity commissioner, ombudsman, auditor general, lobbyist registrar (or registration process), and closed meeting investigation. These functions may be internal to the municipality and report directly to council or may be established by the province territory with responsibility for all municipalities. Municipalities establish special purpose bodies to take on tasks or functions that require specialized attention, like housing, hydro, the zoo, and transit. These corporations are owned by and accountable to the municipality and will have varying authority and autonomy from the municipality. In addition, provinces establish special purpose bodies to oversee or supplement municipalities, or they require the establishment of municipal special purpose bodies in legislation and regulations (e.g., police). Municipal-provincial relationships change over time depending on provincial and local politics and pressures. The informal federal-municipal relationship also changes over time, but it is always mediated by the fact that municipalities aren t a constitutionally recognized form of government. While municipalities continue to be at a disadvantage because of the lack of constitutional standing, there are new multi-governance paths for intergovernmental relationships that work around the lack of constitutional status. Not to be forgotten, municipalities also have longstanding formal and informal relationships with other municipalities. This is an important relationship for both parties, which can be expected to increase in importance in the future. Indigenous peoples are important to municipalities the Indigenous population is growing at a faster rate than non-Indigenous populations, they are younger than average, and they : Week 5 continued to explore the machinery of local government with a focus on the administration or bureaucracy, municipal accountability officers, and municipal special purpose bodies (or agencies, boards, and commissions (A, B, Cs)). : Week 6 focused on the complicated relationship between the municipal, provincial, and federal levels of government in Canada the often grey area of intergovernmental relations. : Week 7 examined municipal-Indigenous relationships. are increasingly urban. In addition, municipalities may be affected by formal land claims settlements negotiated by the federal and provincial territorial governments. The module explored three types of relationships: formal municipal-First Nations agreements, urban reserves, and other accords, protocols, and policy and program initiatives within the It provided some historical perspective on the traditional and imposed First Nations governance as well as examples of self-governance. The module considered municipal expenditures on directly controlled services, downloaded provincial services or regulated services, special purpose bodies, and capital spending. It looked at the three main sources of municipal revenues property taxes, user fees, and grants and funding from other levels of government. The module gave you an opportunity to try the difficult task of balancing a budget making budget decisions in the context of competing priorities and pressures. It highlighted the challenges facing municipalities and helped you to understand the options available to them. Municipalities are described as being in a state of fiscal crisis and the module considered possible responses. There are many internal and external influences on municipal policy; although, we may think of policy in terms of rational processes, it is far from the case in reality. and considered with input from interested stakeholders. Policy options are constrained (or enabled) by municipal formal powers, municipal staff, and financial capacity. Council committees, often with input from interested parties, discuss policy before it is debated and voted on at open council meetings. The module also considered issues related to municipal services, which can be delivered directly by municipal staff, municipal special purpose bodies, or for-profit or nonprofit organizations contracted to provide the services. Over time, municipalities have become more involved in non- traditional and non-property-related service issues, such as homelessness, poverty reduction, and climate change. : Week 8 explored the complex issue of municipal finance by looking at municipal budgets and budget processes. : Week 9 considered the municipal policy-making processes and environment and highlighted that policy-making is a complex process at the local level. : Week 10 considered municipal relationships with the public, including public participation and engagement as well as relationships with service users, citizens , and Many different interests and interest groups business, identity, local community, consumer, staff union, and policy groups try to influence municipal decision-making and service delivery. While there have been many strong efforts at engagement, municipal relationships with the public are not as inclusive or accessible as they need to be to connect effectively with diverse individuals, groups, and communities. Technology and online platforms have the potential for enhanced access and connection and greater levels of engagement. The module ended with a brief look at climate change and local government. As you review the key course learning summary on the previous page, you will no doubt notice some similarities and themes in what other students are taking away from the course as important learnings. Here are some additional thoughts on key course themes: : Week 11 looked at two important issues facing local government today technology and globalization. Both will shape the future path of municipalities within provinces, nationally, and on the international stage. They bring implications for relationships with other levels of government and for relationships with community members. Complex Environment Municipalities operate in complex environments. The structure of municipalities can be complicated with regional, two-tier structures as well as municipal and provincial special purpose bodies. Elected representatives are not associated with political parties, which can make decision-making more robust, but at the same time more complicated and more difficult to predict. Downloading has added complexity associated with provincial program requirements and funding and municipal funding, particularly grants, and is subject to change. In addition to special purpose bodies, municipalities have relationships with the private and nonprofit sectors to deliver services. Municipal issues cross boundaries and are increasingly regional. Technology and globalization add further complexities to the challenging context in which municipalities operate. Change Local government, unlike the constitutionally recognized provincial territorial or federal levels of government, is subject to change. While the foundation is remarkably similar to that of the Baldwin Act, 1849, there have been changes in the structure and responsibilities of municipalities through provincially imposed amalgamations and changes to representation and roles. Municipalities have been able to pursue some structural and governance reforms. However, more frequently, provincial decisions overlook municipal and public opposition to the changes. Relationships Municipalities have formal and informal relationships with the public and many interests, stakeholders, and levels of government. As the level of government closest to the people, these relationships bring high expectations regarding efficiency, effectiveness, representation, and engagement in decision- making processes. Municipalities face significant limitations in a formal relationship with the federal government, even though federal policies frequently have direct implications for municipalities. Municipal relationships with provinces are politically unstable and change happens in unanticipated forms. Relationships with Indigenous governments and communities have become increasingly important to municipalities. Capacity Municipalities have limited financial and jurisdictional capacity to address the increasingly complicated local challenges and issues. The course looked at a number of ways that the lack of financial capacity can be addressed, but action has been limited. Lack of jurisdictional capacity was a recurring theme throughout the course. Although municipalities can engage in multi-level governance, act as eager beavers, and be innovative in relationships with the private sector and civil society, these workarounds take time and effort. Looking Back at the Four Key Tensions Before we leave this review and reflection module, let s reflect on the four tensions from the Introducing Four Key Tensions page in Module 2. These tensions have been evident throughout the course, in the discussions of structure, reforms, finance, delegated roles and authority, interests, representation, service delivery, and policy. 1. Tensions between the municipal service delivery and democratic roles were presented in the discussion of role, structure, and process and repeated in the other tensions below. As urban municipalities continue to grow in population and economic importance, and as issues become increasingly complex, municipalities will need to play a stronger democratic and representative role. 2. Tensions between efficiency and effectiveness were evident in the discussions of municipal role, processes, and finances. Cost efficiency is key if the role of local government is service provision; whereas effectiveness and how municipalities do their work and make decisions is critical to the municipal role of democratic representation. The tension between efficiency and effectiveness isn t either or because local governments need some elements of both; the tension is in the balance between efficiency and effectiveness. 3. Tensions about municipal autonomy and the relationship with provinces and territories have been evident throughout the course from the historical overview early in the course to the last module and the future path for municipalities. The municipal relationship with the federal government is constrained by lack of constitutional autonomy and politics at all levels. 4. Finally, the course explored tensions related to stakeholders and how local governments balance and mediate these interests. Reflecting on the discussion of interests, perhaps we also need to add represent to this tension, i.e., tensions related to representation, interests, and how local governments , to highlight tensions in the selection of elected officials, how they represent the many interests in their communities, and whether they play trustee or delegate roles. We are now at the end of the course. At this point, you have developed an appreciation for the often- overlooked local level of government and now possess a better understanding of the challenges of the current system and structures of governance. You also understand how your municipality is governed and have seen the municipality in action in a decision-making council or committee meeting. You can apply these course learnings to future engagement with your municipality and to your broader understanding of inter-governmental relationships in Canada. In addition, you should also have a good understanding of the many municipal interests and the challenges that municipalities face in trying to balance these varied interests. The course explored a number of issues facing municipalities as well as opportunities for leadership, innovation, and continued strengthening of local government.
