Understand the roles of Information, Motivation, and Behavioural Skills in changing sexual health related behaviour. 2. Understand the roles of Elicitation, Intervention, and Evaluation in applying the IMB model to sexual health promotion activities. 3. Apply the Elicitation, Intervention, and Evaluation framework and the Information, Motivation, and Behavioural Skills model to sexual health promotion activities. 4. Become aware of opportunities for further professional development in the area of human sexuality. Topic Overview The primary objectives of this course have been to encourage a critical understanding of the social context of human sexuality and to introduce key theories and concepts relevant to the study of human sexuality. We have also paid particular attention to how these concepts can be applied to the work of health professionals addressing sexuality issues in their work. As we have examined the developmental tasks of each phase of the life-cycle and topics such as gender, sexual orientation, and disability, it has become evident that sexuality and sexual health are important components of individual and community well-being. One of the outcomes of the gradual transition from a Restrictive to a Permissive sexual ideology in Western culture is that it is increasingly accepted that in order to comprehensively and holistically meet the needs of their constituents, health professionals (e.g., nurses, rehabilitation counsellors, social workers, and educators) must be willing and able to address issues of sexuality. If we are to utilize our knowledge and expertise in human sexuality to the benefit of our students, clients and communities, it is crucial to incorporate effective, well-tested approaches to sexual health promotion in our work. The aim of this final module is to provide you with a basic general approach to developing and implementing effective sexual health promotion activities. Please Note To effectively promote sexual health, health professionals will benefit from using well-tested, behaviourally focused approaches to sexual health enhancement and problem prevention. We are at a unique juncture in history and have a rare opportunity to develop global, national, and community strategies to promote sexual health for the new century. This opportunity has been created by the fact that the world is experiencing a new sexual revolution and a public health imperative. Much like the sexual revolution of the 1960s and 1970s, it is a revolution fuelled by incredible scientific advances, as well as a dramatic social and economic change We also face a myriad of sexual health problems, which is creating an enormous burden on societies. These two factors are putting pressure on health ministries to develop comprehensive approaches to sexual health promotion . The Information, Motivation, Behavioural Skills Model for Sexual and Reproductive Health Promotion In Module 8, Part 2, we saw how the information, motivation, behavioural skills theoretical model can be applied to adolescent sexuality education. Although it was initially developed and tested as a theoretical model to guide interventions targeting HIV AIDS sexual risk reduction, the IMB model can be used to guide a wide range of sexual health promotion and education activities . Whether we are creating and implementing programs to promote sexual health with individuals, groups, or communities, the IMB model provides a basic and well-tested conceptual framework for ensuring that our interventions are effective in reaching their objectives. First and foremost, sexual and reproductive health behaviors, whether they involve sexual function promotion, contraceptive utilization, STD HIV prevention, reproductive cancer screening, or sexual adaptations to aging, illness, or disability, represent sexualized behavioral events. Each of these sexual and reproductive health behaviors has acquired sexual meaning as a result of social ascription, individual learning, and the sexual and reproductive biology and physiology involved in all of the acts and adjustments in question. As sexualized acts, sexual and reproductive behaviors are all influenced by a common set of determinants of sexual behavior, including information, motivation, and behavioral skills factors, and all are potentially modifiable via common intervention pathways focusing on changing these factors in efforts to promote sexual and reproductive health (Fisher and Fisher, 1998, p. s 39-40). The IMB Model for Sexual Health Promotion Acquisition of Information Individual must learn information that is relevant to the practice of sexual and reproductive health behaviour. Development of Motivation Individual must become personally motivated and or socially motivated to practice sexual and reproductive health behaviour. Development of Behavioural Skills Individual must learn the actual behavioural skills to perform sexual and reproductive health behaviour. Although the necessity of including relevant information, motivation, and behavioural skills in sexual health promotion programs seems obvious, very few existing programs actually contain all three elements. In many cases, sexual health education programs have been exclusively information-based and, in particular, have neglected to provide specific behavioural skills training. Key Point The degree to which an individual is able to enact sexual health promoting behaviour will be dependant to a significant extent on their level of relevant information, motivation, and behavioural skills. Case Example An IMB Model Intervention Approach to Assist Women with Sexual Function Difficulties Related to Chemotherapy Treatment for Breast Cancer. Research suggests that difficulty with sexual functioning is one of the most distressing problems for long-term survivors of breast cancer and that in many cases these problems are related to vaginal dryness resulting from chemotherapy treatment (e.g., see Broeckel, Thors, Jacobsen, Small, and Cox, 2002). Therefore, one of the objectives of a sexual health education intervention for this group might be to target information, motivation, and behaviour skills related to the use of vaginal lubrication prior to sexual activity. Information: (Must be relevant, complete, and easy to translate into behaviour) Examples of relevant information: Many women treated for breast cancer experience problems with sexual function. These problems are often associated with vaginal dryness. Methods to relieve vaginal dryness, including the use of lubricants prior to sexual activity, can be used to improve sexual function among women treated for breast cancer. Lubricants (e.g. Astroglide) are available, without prescription, at the pharmacy in the Family Planning section where the condoms are displayed. Motivation: (Must help the recipient develop a positive attitude towards the behaviour by recognizing that she will benefit from engaging in the behaviour personal motivation and that there is some form of external support for engaging in the behaviour social motivation ). Examples of motivational messages: Restoring sexual function and enjoyment can play an important role in regaining an overall sense of well-being following treatment for breast cancer (personal motivation). Applying a lubricant prior to sexual activity can be an effective and easy to use method for helping to restore sexual function (personal motivation). Other women experiencing vaginal dryness report that using lubricants is very helpful and health professionals recommend their use for this purpose (social motivation). Behavioural Skills:(Must include the teaching of the specific behavioural skills needed to initiate and maintain the behaviour). Examples of behavioural skills training: Instruction on how to locate lubricant in the pharmacy. Instruction on the application of vaginal lubrication (encourage practice and experimentation). Discussion and decision-making on when in the sexual script lubricant will be applied. Practice and rehearsal of communication with partner about the use of lubricants (give explicit and clear examples of communication and or incorporate role-playing exercises where participants practice bringing up and discussing the use of lubricants with a partner). Applying the IMB Model: Elicitation, Intervention, Evaluation Effectively applying the IMB model to a sexual health promotion intervention involves a basic three-step process: elicitation, intervention, and evaluation. In the elicitation phase, program planners assess or measure the target audience s information, motivation, and behavioural skills related to a specific set of sexual health behaviours. This can be done through focus groups, assessment interviews with clients, or questionnaires. In other words, conducting elicitation research will allow program planners to identify which aspects of information, motivation, and behavioural skills the target audience is lacking. Instead of creating programs based on our own assumptions or best guesses as to what the target audience needs, conducting elicitation research helps to ensure that an intervention will be relevant and have the desired impact. In the second step , the program is created and implemented based on the information, motivation, behavioural skills deficits weaknesses of the target audience. In the third step, the program is periodically assessed (again using focus groups, interviews, questionnaires, ect) to ensure that the program is successfully impacting on the information, motivation, and behavioural skills of the target audience. This information can be used to refine and improve the intervention. Key Point The comprehensive, systematic application of an IMB-based sexual health promotion program involves a three-step process of elicitation research, intervention, and evaluation. This program of information motivation behavioural skills and elicitation intervention evaluation will give you a basic structural framework for the delivery of effective sexual health promotion activities. Your required reading for this module is a section of the Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Education. This section, titled Key Components provides further description and discussion of the IMB model. The Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Education are a recommended resource support document for health professionals involved in the provision of sexual health education and promotion programs. Key Point The Canadian Guidelines for Sexual Health Education provide support and guidance for the development and implementation of effective sexual health education and promotion initiatives. Opportunities for Further Professional Development in the Area of Sexuality The field of sexology has advanced significantly in recent decades. Parallel to the increasing openness towards sexuality in Western culture, the importance of research on human sexuality and sexual health promotion has been increasingly recognized. In addition, as we have seen throughout this course, addressing sexuality and sexual health issues is also gradually becoming an accepted component of the practice of a wide variety of helping professions. However, despite these advancements, it is also important to be aware that sexology has not yet reached the status of a distinct academic discipline. For example, few universities have sexology departments. Indeed, at present, only one sexology department exists in Canada, located at the University of Quebec at Montreal (UQAM s sexology programs are given in the french language). Several universities in Canada offer programs that enable students to specialize in human sexuality (e.g., Graduate programs in the Department of Family Studies at the University of Guelph; Interdisciplinary Studies in Sexuality, Marriage and the Family at the University of Waterloo). For most students seeking to become specialists in sexuality research, education, counselling or therapy, the most advantagious route to so is to pursue graduate studies in an appropriate related academic program (e.g., nursing, clinical, counselling or educational psychology, medicine, social work, education, ect.) and, to the extent possible, focus one s research and study on sexuality within that program. Many graduate programs have faculty members with an interest in sexology and who teach courses related to sexuality. Obtaining additional credentials in one of these more traditional disciplines also provides a broader qualification than training limited to sexology. This is an important factor to consider in choosing a graduate program. In sum, if you want to become a sexuality educator, become an educator first, if you want to become a medical sexologist, become a physician or nurse first, if you want to become a sex therapist, become a therapist first. In other words, first and foremost, establish your credentials in your discipline as you pursue your focus on sexuality. Becoming a Sex Therapist Technically, there are no licensing bodies in Canada that legally certify a person as a sex therapist. Generally speaking, a well-qualified sex therapist has advanced graduate training in an appropriate discipline such as medicine, psychology, social work, nursing ect. as well as additional specific training in sex therapy. Specific training in sex therapy is available through organizations such as the American Association of Sex Educators, Counselors, and Therapists and the Association of Sex Therapy in Ontario (ASTO: A Final Word This course has been ambitious in its scope and objectives. You have been asked to not only develop a critical understanding of the social context of human sexuality. You have also been asked to acquire a wide-ranging knowledge of sexuality issues and concepts that are crucial to the effective promotion of sexual health. You have taken an important first step in developing the knowledge and skills to make a meaningful contribution to the sexual health of those you are or will be working with. Enhancing the ability of individuals, couples, families, and communities to achieve healthy sexuality, a fundamental component of human well-being, is something to be proud of. Question A4 - Many of the developmental tasks related to adult sexuality that we have examined in this course are focused, in one way or another, on couple communication about the sexual dimension of their relationship. Yet, it seems clear that many, if not most couples, rarely if ever have frank, open discussions about their sexual life. From the choices below, select the one that you think is the single most important factor in discouraging couple communication about sexuality. In western culture, as we grow up, we are socialized to not speak openly about sexual matters. Many couples assume that as they get to know each other, they will gradually, through nonverbal communication, come to understand each other sexually With busy work schedules and or growing children, many couples today are simply to tired to think about sex. If you are too tired to have sex, youre probably too tired to talk about it. Other. (if you are selecting 'Other' please consider what your response would be; this will be of significance when answering the open-ended question). Submit Question B4 - Sexual expression can play an important role in the maintenance of an older person's well-being and this can also be the case for elderly people living in nursing homes. The sexual behaviour of nursing home residents can raise some difficult issues for nursing home staff. For example, family members may not approve of their elderly mother or father being sexually active. There may be questions as to whether a particular resident has the mental capacity to consent to sexual activity or to behave appropriately and responsibly. In some cases, nursing home residents may not have adequate privacy to engage in sexual activity unless they are assisted by nursing home staff. Given these issues, which of the following do you think is the best approach regarding sexual activity of nursing home residents. All nursing homes should have policies that include the recognition that nursing home residents have a basic right to sexual expression just like any other person. This will make life more difficult and complicated for the staff but they have to understand and accept that dealing with these issues is part of the job. Nursing home staff should not be obligated to assist residents with respect to sexual expression (For example, arranging for residents to have the privacy to be alone together). Staff should only become involved in sexuality related matters if residents are behaving inappropriately. other Submit C4 - Sexual expression can play an important role in the well-being of a person with a developmental disability and this can also be the case for those people living in group homes. The sexual behaviour of group home residents can raise some difficult issues for group home staff. For example, some family members may not approve of a son or daughter being sexually active. There may be questions as to whether a particular resident has the mental capacity to consent to sexual activity or to behave appropriately and responsibly. In some cases, group home residents may not have adequate privacy to engage in sexual activity unless they are assisted by group home staff. Given these issues, which of the following do you think is the best approach regarding the sexual behaviour of people with developmental disabilities living in group homes? All group homes for people with developmental disabilities should have policies that include the recognition that residents have a basic right to sexual expression just like any other person. This will make life more difficult and complicated for the staff but they have to understand and accept that dealing with these issues is part of the job. Group home staff should not be obligated to assist residents with respect to sexual expression (For example, arranging for residents to have the privacy to be alone together). Staff should only become involved in sexuality related matters if residents are behaving inappropriately. Other Submit Question D4 - It is very clear from statistics on STIs and unintended pregnancy, that many adolescents and adults are not taking the necessary measures to protect their sexual health. For example, rates of condom use are much lower than they should be for both groups. From the choices below, select the one that you think is the single most important factor that explains the low level of condom use in Canada. Too many people are simply unaware of the risks involved in having unprotected sex. Most people are aware of the risks generally but too many of them believe that they personally are not at risk for HIV or STDs. For example, some people erroneously believe that because they are monogamous (i.e., they currently only have one sexual partner) or that they know their partner well, they are not at risk and, therefore, do not need to use condoms Most people at risk for HIV or STD know that they should be using condoms. However, many of these people are too embarrassed to bring up the subject of condom use with their partner or they are unsure about how to talk about it or they are worried about how their partner might react to the suggestion that condoms should be used.
